use physiome::repair::{Inputs, Perturbation};
use physiome::state::all_violations;
use physiome::{
    all_continuations, all_repair_ops, all_subsystem_specifications, settle, step_until,
    step_until_hierarchical, validate_new_subsystem_specs, ContinuationEntry,
    HierarchicalContinuation, HierarchyLevel, PhysiologicalState, RepairEvent,
};

fn run_scenario(inputs: Inputs, hours: f64, seed: u64) -> (PhysiologicalState, usize) {
    run_scenario_from(PhysiologicalState::baseline(), inputs, hours, seed)
}

fn run_scenario_from(
    state: PhysiologicalState,
    inputs: Inputs,
    hours: f64,
    seed: u64,
) -> (PhysiologicalState, usize) {
    let ops = all_repair_ops();
    let clocks = all_continuations();
    let continuations = clocks.as_vec();
    let perturbation = Perturbation { seed };

    let (final_state, log) = step_until(
        state,
        &continuations,
        &ops,
        &inputs,
        &perturbation,
        hours * 3600.0,
        30,
        all_violations,
    );

    (final_state, log.len())
}

fn run_scenario_with_log(
    state: PhysiologicalState,
    inputs: Inputs,
    hours: f64,
    seed: u64,
) -> (PhysiologicalState, Vec<RepairEvent>) {
    let ops = all_repair_ops();
    let clocks = all_continuations();
    let continuations = clocks.as_vec();
    let perturbation = Perturbation { seed };

    step_until(
        state,
        &continuations,
        &ops,
        &inputs,
        &perturbation,
        hours * 3600.0,
        30,
        all_violations,
    )
}

fn map_after_hours(inputs: Inputs, hours: f64, seed: u64) -> f64 {
    run_scenario(inputs, hours, seed)
        .0
        .cardiovascular
        .mean_arterial_pressure
}

#[test]
fn baseline_state_is_fully_admissible() {
    let state = PhysiologicalState::baseline();
    let violations = all_violations(&state);
    assert!(
        violations.is_empty(),
        "baseline() should start admissible across all subsystems, found: {:?}",
        violations
    );
}

#[test]
fn subsystem_spec_template_exists_and_is_valid() {
    let specs = all_subsystem_specifications();
    assert!(
        specs.len() >= 6,
        "expected template specs for newly added level-1 subsystems"
    );
    validate_new_subsystem_specs().expect("new subsystem coupling contracts should validate");
}

#[test]
fn pathogen_challenge_raises_core_temp_via_cytokine_coupling() {
    let baseline = PhysiologicalState::baseline();
    let inputs = Inputs {
        pathogen_load: 3.0,
        ambient_temp: 22.0,
        ..Default::default()
    };
    let (final_state, log) = run_scenario_with_log(baseline.clone(), inputs, 2.0, 42);

    assert!(
        log.iter()
            .any(|event| event.op_name == "fever_response" && !event.skipped_due_to_conflict),
        "expected fever_response dispatch under sustained pathogen load"
    );
    assert!(
        (final_state.thermal.core_temp - baseline.thermal.core_temp).abs() > 0.05,
        "pathogen challenge should perturb thermal state from baseline"
    );
    assert!(
        final_state.thermal.core_temp < 40.0,
        "fever should remain bounded"
    );
}

#[test]
fn hemorrhage_scenario_invokes_compensation_without_runaway() {
    let inputs = Inputs {
        hemorrhage_rate: 2.0,
        ..Default::default()
    };
    let (final_state, _) = run_scenario(inputs, 1.0, 7);

    assert!(
        final_state.hematologic.hemoglobin > 9.0,
        "hemoglobin should not collapse under modeled compensation"
    );
    assert!(
        final_state.cardiovascular.mean_arterial_pressure > 70.0,
        "pressure should remain in minimally viable range"
    );
}

#[test]
fn heat_stress_scenario_engages_surface_cooling() {
    let baseline = PhysiologicalState::baseline();
    let inputs = Inputs {
        ambient_temp: 34.0,
        ..Default::default()
    };
    let (final_state, _) = run_scenario(inputs, 1.0, 13);

    assert!(
        final_state.integumentary.sweat_rate >= baseline.integumentary.sweat_rate,
        "heat stress should increase sweat output"
    );
    assert!(
        final_state.thermal.core_temp < 39.0,
        "surface cooling should prevent runaway hyperthermia"
    );
}

#[test]
fn metabolic_challenge_consumes_glycogen_and_remains_bounded() {
    let inputs = Inputs {
        exercise_intensity: 0.9,
        meal_glucose_load: 60.0,
        ..Default::default()
    };
    let (final_state, _) = run_scenario(inputs, 1.5, 101);

    assert!(
        final_state.musculoskeletal.glycogen_reserve < 100.0,
        "sustained exertion should consume glycogen reserve"
    );
    assert!(
        (60.0..=300.0).contains(&final_state.metabolic.blood_glucose),
        "blood glucose should stay physiologically bounded in this challenge"
    );
}

#[test]
fn endocrine_stress_shifts_hormonal_axis_without_breaking_admissibility() {
    let inputs = Inputs {
        exercise_intensity: 0.7,
        pathogen_load: 1.2,
        ..Default::default()
    };
    let (final_state, _events) = run_scenario(inputs, 3.0, 88);

    assert!(
        (5.0..=23.0).contains(&final_state.endocrine.cortisol),
        "cortisol should remain within endocrine admissibility bounds"
    );
    let unresolved = all_violations(&final_state);
    assert!(
        unresolved.iter().all(|v| v.subsystem != "endocrine"),
        "endocrine stress run should not leave unresolved endocrine violations: {:?}",
        unresolved
    );
    assert!(
        unresolved.len() <= 20,
        "stress run should reduce unresolved violations, got {}",
        unresolved.len()
    );
}

#[test]
fn nephrectomy_half_mass_uses_scaled_renal_admissibility() {
    let mut state = PhysiologicalState::baseline();
    state.renal.functioning_mass = 0.5;
    state.renal.gfr = 52.0;

    let (final_state, _) = run_scenario_from(state, Inputs::default(), 3.0, 2026);
    let unresolved = all_violations(&final_state);

    assert!(
        final_state.renal.gfr < 90.0,
        "half-mass renal state should not be forced to healthy two-kidney GFR"
    );
    assert!(
        unresolved.iter().all(|v| {
            !(v.subsystem == "renal"
                && v.variable == "gfr"
                && (45.0..=60.0).contains(&final_state.renal.gfr))
        }),
        "scaled renal boundary should treat half-mass GFR as admissible, got {:?}",
        unresolved
    );
}

#[test]
fn renal_artery_stenosis_decouples_local_perfusion_from_systemic_pressure() {
    let baseline = PhysiologicalState::baseline();
    let inputs = Inputs {
        renal_artery_stenosis: 0.45,
        ..Default::default()
    };
    let (final_state, _) = run_scenario(inputs, 2.0, 99);

    assert!(
        final_state.renal.perceived_perfusion_pressure
            < final_state.cardiovascular.mean_arterial_pressure,
        "stenosis should make local renal perfusion lower than systemic MAP"
    );
    assert!(
        final_state.cardiovascular.mean_arterial_pressure >= baseline.cardiovascular.mean_arterial_pressure,
        "RAAS compensation under stenosis should avoid systemic hypotension in this sketch"
    );
}

#[test]
fn renal_artery_stenosis_map_reaches_bounded_plateau() {
    let baseline = PhysiologicalState::baseline();
    let inputs = Inputs {
        renal_artery_stenosis: 0.45,
        ..Default::default()
    };

    let map_2h = map_after_hours(inputs.clone(), 2.0, 99);
    let map_4h = map_after_hours(inputs.clone(), 4.0, 99);
    let map_5h = map_after_hours(inputs, 5.0, 99);

    assert!(
        map_2h >= baseline.cardiovascular.mean_arterial_pressure,
        "stenosis run should avoid dropping below baseline MAP by 2h"
    );
    assert!(map_5h <= 120.0, "stenosis MAP should remain bounded");
    assert!(
        (map_5h - map_4h).abs() <= 2.0,
        "late-horizon MAP should approach a plateau (4h={:.2}, 5h={:.2})",
        map_4h,
        map_5h
    );
}

#[test]
fn renal_artery_stenosis_engages_raas_pathway() {
    let baseline = PhysiologicalState::baseline();
    let inputs = Inputs {
        renal_artery_stenosis: 0.45,
        ..Default::default()
    };
    let (final_state, log) = run_scenario_with_log(baseline.clone(), inputs, 5.0, 99);

    assert!(
        final_state.renal.perceived_perfusion_pressure
            < final_state.cardiovascular.mean_arterial_pressure,
        "stenosis should keep local renal perfusion below systemic MAP"
    );
    assert!(
        final_state.renal.gfr < baseline.renal.gfr,
        "stenosis should reduce GFR from baseline over a 5h run"
    );
    assert!(
        log.iter()
            .any(|event| event.op_name == "raas" && !event.skipped_due_to_conflict),
        "stenosis run should dispatch at least one non-conflicted RAAS repair event"
    );
}

----- FILE: README.md -----
[size: 4812 bytes | lines: 61]

[Physiome](https://standardgalactic.github.io/physiome/physiome.pdf)

An admissibility- and repair-driven whole-body physiology simulator, written in Rust with a pure functional core.

Rather than modeling homeostasis as a fixed system of coupled differential equations (the approach taken by HumMod and similar simulators), physiome treats each physiological variable as carrying an *admissibility boundary*: a range of values the rest of the body will accept as valid. When a variable drifts outside its boundary, the engine dispatches *repair operators*, pure state transitions representing the corrective physiology (baroreflex, RAAS, thermoregulation, and so on) that pull it back. Subsystems run on independent clocks rather than one global timestep, since cardiovascular reflexes, hormonal regulation, and hematopoiesis do not share a timescale.

## Structure

```
src/
  lib.rs           module wiring; all_repair_ops(), all_continuations()
  constraint.rs     Constraint, AdmissibilityBoundary, ObservableBoundary (domain-independent engine)
  repair.rs         RepairOp, step(), settle(), Continuation trait, step_until() scheduler (domain-independent engine)
  state.rs          PhysiologicalState struct-of-structs, baseline(), all_violations()
  specification.rs  subsystem template metadata + coupling contract checks
  subsystems/
    cardiovascular.rs, renal.rs, hepatic.rs, gi.rs, nervous.rs,
    immune.rs, hematologic.rs, endocrine.rs, metabolic.rs,
    respiratory.rs, thermal.rs, microcirculation.rs, lymphatic.rs,
    musculoskeletal.rs, integumentary.rs, skeletal.rs, reproductive.rs
examples/
  infection_scenario.rs, hemorrhage_scenario.rs, heat_stress_scenario.rs,
  metabolic_challenge_scenario.rs, endocrine_stress_scenario.rs
tests/
  infection_scenario_test.rs   baseline admissibility + stress + nephrectomy/stenosis/Ang II invariants
```

## Design

Every subsystem implements two traits:

- `ObservableBoundary` exposes the subsystem's current observable variables and their admissible ranges, so the repair engine can iterate over subsystems uniformly without knowing anything domain-specific about any of them.
- `Continuation` declares how often the subsystem wants to be advanced (`interval`) and how to advance it (`advance`). The scheduler in `step_until` always advances whichever subsystem's next-fire time is soonest, rather than forcing every subsystem onto one global timestep.

A `RepairOp` is data, not a branch in the engine: a name, a predicate for which violations it responds to, and a pure `State -> State` transition. Adding a new corrective pathway means adding a new `RepairOp` value; the dispatch loop in `repair::step` never changes. Multiple ops can respond to the same violation (an elevated cytokine level triggers both `immune_resolution`, pulling it back down, and `fever_response`, raising `thermal::core_temp`); `step` applies every matching op, not just the first.

Cross-subsystem coupling happens only through the shared `PhysiologicalState`: a repair op can read another subsystem's field (hematologic's coagulation response reads hepatic's `clotting_factors`) and write to a third (immune's fever_response writes to thermal's `core_temp`), but subsystem modules never call each other directly.

State-dependent admissibility is supported where physiology demands it: thermal admissibility shifts with cytokine-driven fever set-point, and renal admissibility now scales with `renal.functioning_mass` so nephrectomy-like scenarios can degrade gracefully without being judged against two-kidney GFR bounds.

## Running it

```bash
cargo build
cargo run --example infection_scenario
cargo run --example hemorrhage_scenario
cargo run --example heat_stress_scenario
cargo run --example metabolic_challenge_scenario
cargo run --example endocrine_stress_scenario
cargo test
```

The examples run reproducible stress scenarios from a healthy baseline and print final state summaries. The tests assert baseline admissibility, validate subsystem specification/coupling contracts, and enforce scenario-level physiological invariants.

## Current subsystems

Seventeen are implemented: cardiovascular, renal, hepatic, gastrointestinal, nervous (autonomic), immune, hematologic, endocrine, metabolic, respiratory, thermal, microcirculation, lymphatic, musculoskeletal, integumentary, skeletal, and reproductive. Each carries its own admissible ranges, at least one repair operator, and its own clock.

## Status and roadmap

This is an early architectural sketch, not a validated physiology model. The full project motivation, architecture rationale, implementation details, experimental results (including three real bugs found and fixed during development), and a roadmap toward a complete organ-system specification are written up in `docs/physiome.tex`.

----- END FILE: README.md -----

#!/usr/bin/env bash

# =============================================================================
# icepick.sh
#
# Create a compact repository snapshot for LLM analysis.
#
# Usage:
#   ./icepick.sh
#   ./icepick.sh snapshot.txt
#   ./icepick.sh snapshot.txt /path/to/repo
#
# Options:
#   -o, --output FILE       Output file
#   -r, --root DIR          Repository root
#   -l, --max-lines N       Max lines per normal file
#   -s, --max-size-kb N     Large-file threshold
#   -d, --max-depth N       Tree depth
#   -g, --git               Include Git metadata
#       --hidden            Include hidden files
#       --full              Never truncate text files
#       --no-tree           Do not include directory tree
#       --dry-run           Print files that would be included
#       --include EXT       Force extension inclusion
#       --exclude GLOB      Additional exclusion
#   -q, --quiet             Suppress status messages
#   -h, --help              Show help
#
# =============================================================================

set -u
set -o pipefail

OUTPUT="icepick_snapshot.txt"
ROOT="."

MAX_LINES=300
MAX_SIZE_KB=128
MAX_DEPTH=20

INCLUDE_GIT=0
INCLUDE_HIDDEN=0
FULL_MODE=0
INCLUDE_TREE=1
DRY_RUN=0
QUIET=0

EXTRA_EXTENSIONS=()
EXTRA_EXCLUDES=()

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Helpers
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

log() {
    if (( QUIET == 0 )); then
        printf '%s\n' "$*"
    fi
}

die() {
    printf 'icepick: error: %s\n' "$*" >&2
    exit 1
}

usage() {
    cat <<'EOF'
Icepick — repository snapshot generator for LLM analysis

Usage:

    ./icepick.sh [OUTPUT [ROOT]]
    ./icepick.sh [OPTIONS]

Options:

    -o, --output FILE
    -r, --root DIR
    -l, --max-lines N
    -s, --max-size-kb N
    -d, --max-depth N
    -g, --git
        --hidden
        --full
        --no-tree
        --dry-run
        --include EXT
        --exclude GLOB
    -q, --quiet
    -h, --help

Examples:

    ./icepick.sh

    ./icepick.sh snapshot.txt

    ./icepick.sh snapshot.txt ~/physiome

    ./icepick.sh \
        --root ~/physiome \
        --output physiome.txt \
        --git

    ./icepick.sh \
        --include rs \
        --include tex \
        --max-lines 500
EOF
}

command_exists() {
    command -v "$1" >/dev/null 2>&1
}

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Arguments
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

POSITIONAL=()

while (( $# > 0 )); do
    case "$1" in

        -o|--output)
            [[ $# -ge 2 ]] || die "$1 requires an argument"
            OUTPUT="$2"
            shift 2
            ;;

        -r|--root)
            [[ $# -ge 2 ]] || die "$1 requires an argument"
            ROOT="$2"
            shift 2
            ;;

        -l|--max-lines)
            [[ $# -ge 2 ]] || die "$1 requires an argument"
            MAX_LINES="$2"
            shift 2
            ;;

        -s|--max-size-kb)
            [[ $# -ge 2 ]] || die "$1 requires an argument"
            MAX_SIZE_KB="$2"
            shift 2
            ;;

        -d|--max-depth)
            [[ $# -ge 2 ]] || die "$1 requires an argument"
            MAX_DEPTH="$2"
            shift 2
            ;;

        -g|--git)
            INCLUDE_GIT=1
            shift
            ;;

        --hidden)
            INCLUDE_HIDDEN=1
            shift
            ;;

        --full)
            FULL_MODE=1
            shift
            ;;

        --no-tree)
            INCLUDE_TREE=0
            shift
            ;;

        --dry-run)
            DRY_RUN=1
            shift
            ;;

        --include)
            [[ $# -ge 2 ]] || die "$1 requires an extension"
            EXTRA_EXTENSIONS+=("${2#.}")
            shift 2
            ;;

        --exclude)
            [[ $# -ge 2 ]] || die "$1 requires a glob"
            EXTRA_EXCLUDES+=("$2")
            shift 2
            ;;

        -q|--quiet)
            QUIET=1
            shift
            ;;

        -h|--help)
            usage
            exit 0
            ;;

        --)
            shift
            while (( $# > 0 )); do
                POSITIONAL+=("$1")
                shift
            done
            ;;

        -*)
            die "unknown option: $1"
            ;;

        *)
            POSITIONAL+=("$1")
            shift
            ;;
    esac
done

if (( ${#POSITIONAL[@]} >= 1 )); then
    OUTPUT="${POSITIONAL[0]}"
fi

if (( ${#POSITIONAL[@]} >= 2 )); then
    ROOT="${POSITIONAL[1]}"
fi

if (( ${#POSITIONAL[@]} > 2 )); then
    die "too many positional arguments"
fi

[[ "$MAX_LINES" =~ ^[0-9]+$ ]] ||
    die "MAX_LINES must be an integer"

[[ "$MAX_SIZE_KB" =~ ^[0-9]+$ ]] ||
    die "MAX_SIZE_KB must be an integer"

[[ "$MAX_DEPTH" =~ ^[0-9]+$ ]] ||
    die "MAX_DEPTH must be an integer"

[[ -d "$ROOT" ]] ||
    die "directory does not exist: $ROOT"

ROOT="$(cd "$ROOT" && pwd -P)"

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Resolve output path
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

if [[ "$OUTPUT" = /* ]]; then
    OUTPUT_ABS="$OUTPUT"
else
    OUTPUT_ABS="$(pwd -P)/$OUTPUT"
fi

mkdir -p "$(dirname "$OUTPUT_ABS")"

OUTPUT_DIR="$(cd "$(dirname "$OUTPUT_ABS")" && pwd -P)"
OUTPUT_ABS="$OUTPUT_DIR/$(basename "$OUTPUT_ABS")"

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Temporary file list
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TMP_FILE="$(mktemp)"

cleanup() {
    rm -f "$TMP_FILE"
}

trap cleanup EXIT INT TERM

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# File classification
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

is_self() {
    local file="$1"
    local candidate

    candidate="$(cd "$(dirname "$file")" 2>/dev/null && pwd -P)/$(basename "$file")"

    [[ "$candidate" == "$OUTPUT_ABS" ]]
}

is_excluded_directory_path() {
    local file="$1"

    case "$file" in
        */.git/*)          return 0 ;;
        */.svn/*)          return 0 ;;
        */.hg/*)           return 0 ;;
        */node_modules/*)  return 0 ;;
        */target/*)        return 0 ;;
        */dist/*)          return 0 ;;
        */build/*)         return 0 ;;
        */coverage/*)      return 0 ;;
        */.next/*)         return 0 ;;
        */.nuxt/*)         return 0 ;;

MATCH_FILE="$(mktemp)"

############################################
# Interactive affliction/infliction disambiguation
#
# Scanning happens on .txt only (the source transcripts). When a match
# is confirmed as the name ("n"), the same word swap is propagated by
# context match into any sibling .vtt/.srt/.json files that share the
# same basename, since those were generated together from the same text.
############################################

echo "---- Interactive disambiguation: affliction / infliction (txt only, propagated to vtt/srt/json) ----"

while IFS= read -r -d '' file; do
  python3 - "$file" "$LOG_FILE" <<'PY'
import sys
import re
import os

filename = sys.argv[1]
log_file = sys.argv[2]
pattern = re.compile(r'\b([Aa]ffliction|[Ii]nfliction|[Ff]iction)\b')

try:
    tty = open("/dev/tty")
except Exception:
    sys.exit(0)

def ask(prompt):
    print(prompt, end='', flush=True)
    return tty.readline().strip().lower()

try:
    with open(filename, "r", encoding="utf-8", errors="ignore") as f:
        lines = f.readlines()
except Exception:
    sys.exit(0)

modified = False
log_entries = []
quit_requested = False

base, _ = os.path.splitext(filename)
sibling_exts = (".vtt", ".srt", ".json")
sibling_files = [base + ext for ext in sibling_exts if os.path.exists(base + ext)]

# Tracks how far into each sibling file we've already consumed, so that
# repeated identical context phrases don't all collapse onto the first hit.
sibling_cursor = {}

def propagate(matched_word, context_words, replacement_word):
    if not sibling_files or not context_words:
        return
    ctx_pattern = re.compile(r'\s+'.join(re.escape(w) for w in context_words), re.IGNORECASE)
    word_pattern = re.compile(re.escape(matched_word), re.IGNORECASE)

    for sib in sibling_files:
        try:
            with open(sib, "r", encoding="utf-8", errors="ignore") as sf:
                content = sf.read()
        except Exception:
            continue

        start = sibling_cursor.get(sib, 0)
        m = ctx_pattern.search(content, start)
        if not m:
            m = ctx_pattern.search(content)  # fall back to a full-file search
        if not m:
            log_entries.append(f"  [propagate] no match in {sib} for context: {' '.join(context_words)}")
            continue

        span_text = m.group(0)
        new_span = word_pattern.sub(replacement_word, span_text, count=1)
        if new_span == span_text:
            continue

        new_content = content[:m.start()] + new_span + content[m.end():]
        sibling_cursor[sib] = m.start() + len(new_span)

        with open(sib, "w", encoding="utf-8") as sf:
            sf.write(new_content)
        log_entries.append(f"  [propagate] {sib}: \"{span_text}\" -> \"{new_span}\"")

for i, line in enumerate(lines):
    if pattern.search(line):
        print("\n---")
        print(f"{filename}:{i+1}")
        print(line.strip())

        while True:
            resp = ask("Is this the name (n) or the concept (c)? [n/c/skip/quit]: ")
            if resp in ("n", "c", "skip", "quit"):
                break

        if resp == "quit":
            quit_requested = True
            break

        if resp == "n":
            original = line.strip()
            match = pattern.search(line)
            matched_word = match.group(0)

            words = line.split()
            target_idx = None
            for idx, w in enumerate(words):
                if matched_word.lower() in w.lower():
                    target_idx = idx
                    break

            if target_idx is not None:
                context_before = words[max(0, target_idx - 3):target_idx]
                context_after = words[target_idx + 1:target_idx + 4]
                context_words = context_before + [words[target_idx]] + context_after
            else:
                context_words = [matched_word]

            lines[i] = pattern.sub("Flyxion", line)
            modified = True
            log_entries.append(f"  L{i+1}: {original}")
            log_entries.append(f"        -> {lines[i].strip()}")

            propagate(matched_word, context_words, "Flyxion")
        else:
            log_entries.append(f"  L{i+1}: kept as-is [{resp}]: {line.strip()}")

if modified:
    with open(filename, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        f.writelines(lines)
    print(f"Updated (affliction/infliction pass): {filename}")

if log_entries:
    with open(log_file, "a", encoding="utf-8") as lf:
        lf.write(f"\n[affliction/infliction] {filename}\n")
        lf.write("\n".join(log_entries) + "\n")

if quit_requested:
    print("Disambiguation stopped early for this file (quit).")
PY
done < <(find . -type f -name "*.txt" ! -path "./backup_*/*" -print0)

############################################
# Vim script
############################################

cat > "$VIM_SCRIPT" <<EOF
let g:flyxion_matches = []
g/\\v\\c(^|[^A-Za-z])(${PATTERN})([^A-Za-z]|$)/call add(g:flyxion_matches, line('.') . "\\x01" . getline('.'))
g/\\v\\cMediaCoins/call add(g:flyxion_matches, line('.') . "\\x01" . getline('.'))
g/\\v\\coblocosm/call add(g:flyxion_matches, line('.') . "\\x01" . getline('.'))
call writefile(g:flyxion_matches, '${MATCH_FILE}')
%s/\\v\\c(^|[^A-Za-z])(${PATTERN})([^A-Za-z]|$)/\\1${REPLACEMENT}\\3/g
%s/\\v\\c(in|of|af|am|an|at|to)(flyxion)/\\1 Flyxion/g
%s/\\v\\coblocosm/oblicosm/g
%s/\\v\\cMediaCoins/Media Quines/g
%s/\\v\\bsphere[ ]+pop\\b/spherepop/g
g/\v\c<amplit[- ]?wist>/call add(g:flyxion_matches, line('.') . "\x01" . getline('.'))
g/\v\c<amplitwist>/call add(g:flyxion_matches, line('.') . "\x01" . getline('.'))
%s/\v\c<amplit[- ]?wist>/amplitwist/g
%s/\v\c<Zeem's scaled>/zoom-scaled/g
%s/\v\c<Happo Praxis>/Haplopraxis/g
%s/\v\c<haphopraxis>/Haplopraxis/g
%s/\v\c<Mathpetitions>/mathematicians/g
wq!
EOF

############################################
# Main normalization pass
############################################

while IFS= read -r -d '' file; do
  case "$file" in
    *.json|*.srt|*.tsv|*.txt|*.vtt)

      FILES_SCANNED=$((FILES_SCANNED + 1))

      before_hash="$(sha256sum "$file" | awk '{print $1}')"

      : > "$MATCH_FILE"
      vim -Es "$file" -S "$VIM_SCRIPT" >/dev/null 2>&1 || true

      after_hash="$(sha256sum "$file" | awk '{print $1}')"

      if [ "$before_hash" != "$after_hash" ]; then
        FILES_UPDATED=$((FILES_UPDATED + 1))
        match_count=0

        {
          echo ""
          echo "[substitution] $file"
        } >> "$LOG_FILE"

        if [ -s "$MATCH_FILE" ]; then
          while IFS=$'\x01' read -r lineno before_text; do
            [ -z "$lineno" ] && continue
            printf '  L%s: %s\n' "$lineno" "$before_text" >> "$LOG_FILE"
            match_count=$((match_count + 1))
          done < "$MATCH_FILE"
        fi

        echo "  ($match_count line(s) changed)" >> "$LOG_FILE"
        echo "Updated: $file"
      else
        FILES_UNCHANGED=$((FILES_UNCHANGED + 1))
      fi

      python3 -c '
import sys, difflib, re

target = "flyxion"
threshold = 0.60
filename = sys.argv[1]

prefixes = (
    "fl", "fly", "fli", "fle", "flu",
    "flek", "flex", "flix", "flux",
    "flyk", "flet", "flic", "flit"
)

try:
    with open(filename, "r", encoding="utf-8", errors="ignore") as fh:
        for line in fh:
            for w in re.findall(r"[A-Za-z]{5,18}", line):
                lw = w.lower()

                if lw == target:
                    continue

                if not lw.startswith(prefixes):
                    continue

                score = difflib.SequenceMatcher(None, lw, target).ratio()

                if score >= threshold or any(x in lw for x in ("xion", "xian", "xen", "zion", "ksh", "shun", "shion")):
                    print(lw)
except Exception:
    pass
' "$file" >> "$CANDIDATE_FILE"

      ;;
  esac
done < <(find . -type f ! -path "./backup_*/*" -print0)

############################################
# Candidate aggregation
############################################

We're talking about gene regulation and intracellular signaling pathways. Systems that operate on timescales of milliseconds or even microseconds. Exactly. If you introduce a cellular subsystem that requests to be advanced every single millisecond, doesn't that cripple the entire engine? Because even though the liver isn't being advanced every millisecond, the central scheduler still has to run its detection loop to check for violations every time any clock ticks. Yeah, you are hitting on a massive computational bottleneck, which the author explicitly acknowledges in their discussion of big O complexity. Let's talk about big O. Yeah, so the detection phase of the Physiome engine, where it scans all the active subsystems to evaluate their miscibility boundary constraints, is an ON operation. Which means the computational cost scales linearly with the number of active constraints. Right, so if you have 10 subsystems, the CPU does 10 units of work to check the boundaries. If you have 100 subsystems, it takes 100 units of work. But the critical factor is frequency, isn't it? It is entirely about frequency. Because the scheduler must rerun this complete ON detection sweep after every single tick, the total CPU cost of running a simulation for an hour is entirely dominated by the fastest clocked subsystem in the ledger. So if you introduce a molecular system operating at 1,000 ticks per second, the engine is suddenly forced to run that ON organism-wide boundary check millions of times a minute. Yeah. The CPU cache would be overwhelmed just checking to see if the slow systems were still okay millions of times over. Exactly. Flyxion notes that the cost of these ON sweeps must be weighed heavily before integrating sub-second systems. Decentralized time solves the mathematical rigidity of ODEs, but it introduces a severe performance penalty if you have extreme disparities in your clock speeds. And as the paper details, this asynchronous clock system wasn't just some theoretical challenge. When the author actually booted up the engine to simulate an infection, this exact timing architecture spectacularly broke the digital organism. Oh yeah, I was a mess. This is where the paper reads less like an academic journal and more like a true crime postmortem for software engineers. It really does. Let's walk through the four-hour infection scenario because the bugs they encountered perfectly illustrate why this architecture is so unforgiving yet so valuable. Sure. So the testing scenario was straightforward. The author instantiated a healthy digital organism and subjected it to a sustained exogenous pathogen load. A severe infection. Right. Physiologically, the expectation is that this pathogen load will drive up white blood cells, C-reactive protein, and various cytokines within the immune subsystem. And the expected mechanical response from the engine is that the immunoresolution operator will detect the cytokine violation and begin fighting it, slowly pulling those numbers down. But if the pathogen load is too aggressive and the cytokines breach a critical upper boundary, the engine should also trigger the fever response operator. Yes. And the fever response is designed to raise the core body temperature to assist in neutralizing the pathogen. And critically, this operator is programmed with a hard physiological cap. What's the cap? The mathematics of the pure function dictate that it should never push the core body temperature beyond 41.5 degrees Celsius, regardless of how severe the underlying cytokine violation becomes. That is the design intent. Yep. But when they actually ran the four-hour simulation, the data output revealed three massive cascading defects that effectively destroy the simulated organism. Let's dissect them. Let's start with bug number one. Okay. So we can categorize the first defect as the monopolizing clock. The monopolizing clock. As we discussed, the scheduler maintains a ledger of when each subsystem should fire. In the initial build, the logic for updating that ledger was mathematically flawed. Oh, so? When a subsystem fired, the scheduler calculated its next fire time by taking the current global simulation time and simply adding the subsystem's fixed interval. Which seems logical at first glance. Yeah. Like, if the global timer is at 10 seconds and my interval is 5 seconds, my next fire time is 15 seconds. It seems logical, but because the scheduler recomputed this basic equation dynamically on every single iteration of the loop, the sheer density of the fast clocks broke the logic. Oh, I see where this is going. Yeah. The cardiovascular system's one-second clock was always placed at the absolute front of the priority queue. It fired, updated the global time by one second, and rescheduled itself. The slower systems were constantly evaluated against a global time that was inching forward in one-second increments. Resulting in complete starvation for the slow systems. Yes. The cardiovascular system effectively cut in line in perpetuity. Precisely. After running the simulation for four in-game hours, the system logs showed that literally zero immune repair events had occurred. Zero. None. The immune subsystem, operating on a five-minute clock, was never granted permission by the schedule to advance. It sat frozen while the heart beat thousands of times. Wow. Yeah. They had to rewrite the scheduler's bookkeeping algorithm to strictly track independent elapsed time per subsystem, ensuring priority was based on true deficit rather than dynamic global addition. Okay, so they fixed the scheduler. The immune system is finally allowed to wake up and recognize the massive pathogen load that has been accumulating for four hours. All right. And that sounds the alarm, which leads directly to bug number two, the directional defect. Ah, yes. This is the bug that resulted in the simulated organism reaching a core temperature of over 10 million degrees. Okay, 10 million degrees. How does the code even allow that to happen? Well, the engine dispatched an operator named leukocytosis, which was registered in the library as the correct physiological response to an elevated white blood cell count. Okay, so far so good. But the mathematical implementation inside that operator's pure function was written backward. Oh, no. Instead of subtracting from the white blood cell count to repair the violation and bring it back down toward the boundary, the function added to it. So the alarm goes off saying, white blood cells are dangerously high. And the dispatch repair operator replies, understood, I will increase them further. Exactly. And because the engine loops continuously, it re-evaluated the state on the next tick. The violation was now even worse, so the severity score was higher. Oh, boy. It dispatched the same broken operator again, which added even more. It created an infinite compounding runaway feedback loop. Because the violation was never mathematically resolved, the operator fired on every single tick without bound, actively driving the system into catastrophic failure. But wait, didn't you say the fever response operator had a hard cap of 41.5 degrees? How did the temperature reach 10 million? Ah, that introduces the third defect, the dispatch short circuit. But what is that? Remember when we discussed how the engine must apply multiple repairs sequentially using sequential composition? Yeah. Well, the author hadn't fully implemented that yet. The search function was still utilizing a find-first logic. Ah. It found the broken leukocytosis operator first, applied it, and stopped looking. Exactly. The engine never even reached the fever response operator in the library. The hard cap of 41.5 degrees was never enforced because the code containing that rule was never executed. The backwards operator monopolized the dispatch phase just as the one-second clock had monopolized the scheduler. I have to ask the obvious question here. If a clinician or a pharmaceutical developer is reviewing this architecture as a potential tool, right, and they see that a simple mathematical typo can cook a digital human to 10 million degrees in a matter of seconds, why would they ever trust this system? It's a fair question. Because in a clinical setting, that level of volatility seems unacceptable. It is entirely counterintuitive, I know, but from a software engineering perspective, the sheer absurdity of the 10 million degree failure is actually the strongest argument for the architecture. Think about how you would have to debug this in HEMAD. Did the temperature spike because of a flaw in the cardiovascular equation? The renal feedback loop? The mathematical stiffness of the matrix integration itself? You have to unravel the entire web to find the source. The failure is systemic. The bug is hidden in the interconnected math. Exactly. But in Physiome, the engine itself performed flawlessly. Flawless. Yeah. The scheduler advanced the clock correctly. The severity function correctly identified a massive violation. The dispatch loop correctly found the operator that claimed it could fix it. The architecture didn't crash. It didn't throw a cryptic memory leak and silently corrupt the data. Oh, I see. It executed the faulty instructions with terrifying precision and logged every single step. Which means isolating the bug took zero effort. Exactly. You look at the log, you see that leukocytosis fired 10,000 times in a row. You open that one isolated file, change the plus sign to a minus sign, and the entire organism is fixed. The structural boundaries of the architecture quarantine human error to highly localized, easily solvable domains. That is amazing. And that level of strict quarantine, the ability to isolate every variable and operator without them secretly entangling, is entirely dependent on the specific programming language the author chose to build this in. Yes. Which transitions us perfectly to the underlying code itself. The paper emphasizes that this architecture is intrinsically tied to the Rust programming language. The choice of Rust is arguably the most technically consequential decision in the entire paper. Really? Oh, yeah. The author leverages Rust to act as a sort of compiler-level immune system for the simulator. The architecture heavily relies on the absence of interior mutability and the enforcement of a pure functional core. Let's break down interior mutability for the engineers listening, because this solves one of the oldest headaches in object-oriented programming. So if you build a simulator in C++ or Python, you often pass objects around by reference. If the cardiovascular module needs to know the state of the liver, you pass a pointer or a reference of the liver object to the cardiovascular function. But in those traditional languages, unless you are meticulously careful with your architecture, that cardiovascular function can use that reference to secretly alter the internal data of the liver object. The cardiovascular module can just spontaneously decide to set the liver's toxin level to zero, and the liver's own internal API wouldn't even know who'd get it. The state is mutated from the inside. Yes. It creates invisible side effects. And when you are trying to simulate a complex biological system, hidden state mutations destroy the integrity of the model. Because you don't know what's happening. You can never truly trust how the organism arrived at its current state. Rust's compiler fundamentally prevents this through its borrow checker and strict ownership rules. So how does Physiome structure its data to appease the Rust compiler? In Physiome, every time the state needs to change, whether a clock is advancing or a repair operator is firing, the function takes the current global state of the organism as a strictly immutable reference. Meaning read-only. Yes, read-only. The function analyzes that read-only state, calculates the necessary changes, and then must return a completely new, discrete state object by value. So it doesn't modify the existing organism. It essentially clones it, applies the repair, and outputs organism version 2.0. Exactly. The history of the state transitions is purely preserved, and this strict architectural rule unlocks the most powerful analytical feature of the simulator. Which is? Deterministic replay. Oh, this concept is incredibly familiar to anyone who works with game engines or lockstep network simulations. Because the underlying principle is identical. In Physiome, because there is absolutely no interior mutability, no hidden side effects, and no random ambient inputs being injected mid-simulation, the execution of the model is mathematically deterministic. Which means, if I record a simulation of a patient undergoing a severe allergic reaction over the course of three hours, I don't need to save a massive terabyte-sized database of every variable at every millisecond to review the footage later. No. You only need to save two things. The exact initial state of the organism at time zero, and a tiny text file logging the specific inputs you introduced, like injecting a simulated dose of epinephrine at minute 45. And when I hit play, the engine rebuilds the universe from that initial seed, feeds it that one input, and because the pure functions are perfectly deterministic, the exact same allergic reaction happens down to the exact millisecond every single time. Think about the implications of that for medical diagnostics. Imagine if an ICU could record the initial blood panel and telemetry of a crashing patient. If the patient's condition deteriorates, the clinical team wouldn't have to guess what cascading failure happened first. They could take that initial state, run it through the deterministic replay of the physiome engine, and perfectly trace the biological failure down to the exact boundary violation that triggered the collapse. Exactly. And because of that strict data isolation, the architecture inherently supports what the paper formalizes as Proposition 3, engine locality. Engine locality. Tell me about that. Engine locality is the guarantee that the domain-independent core, the detect, rank, dispatch loop, will never need to be modified just because you add new biological domains. All right. The author proved this by expanding the initial code base from a simple two-system sketch into an 11-system model without touching a single line of the central engine logic. Because the subsystems are forced to communicate through the shared immutable state rather than directly coupling their equations. Exactly. If the hematologic system needs to calculate blood coagulation, it requires data from the liver. But the hematologic module does not send a request to the hepatic module. Right. It simply reads the clotting factors parameter from the shared read-only organism state, during its turn. This decoupled reading ensures that adding a new organ never breaks the communication pathways of the existing ones. It is entirely modular. You are plugging discrete Lego bricks into a central base plate rather than trying to sculpt a single continuous object out of clay. That's a great way to put it. And this modularity sets the stage for the final section of the paper where Flyxion outlines a comprehensive roadmap for building the rest of the human body. Yeah, the paper details a three-level roadmap for achieving a complete whole-body simulation. Okay, what's level one? Level one focuses on completing the lumped organ scale systems. They already have 11, but they need to integrate the musculoskeletal system, the lymphatic network, and uniquely, the reproductive system. The reproductive system poses a fascinating architectural challenge, doesn't it? Based on everything we just discussed about decentralized time. It is the ultimate stress test for the asynchronous scheduler. You are introducing a biological process dictated by a 28-day clock into an engine that is simultaneously processing a one-second cardiovascular reflex. Wow. Yeah. The engine must flawlessly manage that massive temporal disparity without allowing the fast clocks to monopolize the cue as they did in the early infection models. And once the organ scale is complete, the roadmap moves to level two, which the author labels refinement. Level two shifts from adding new organs to increasing the resolution of existing ones. For instance, instead of treating the renal system as a single black box that filters blood, level two breaks it down into specific nephron segments. Like the proximal tubule, the loop of Henle. Right. It resolves the endocrine system into specific feedback axes, like the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis regulating cortisol. This is also where the paper notes the integration of pharmacokinetics, right? Yes. The introduction of synthetic drugs. Because the system is built on bounds and repairs, a pharmacological intervention is simply modeled as a synthetic repair operator introduced into the library or a modifier to an existing admissibility boundary. That makes total sense. Yeah. And finally, level three pushes the simulation down to the cellular and molecular scale. This involves gene regulation networks and intracellular signaling. As we discussed with Big O complexity, this is where the computational cost of the Owen detection sweep becomes the primary barrier to entry. Implementing level three will require significant optimizations to how the scheduler evaluates constraints. But beyond the technical roadmap, the conclusion of this paper solidifies a profound philosophical argument about the nature of life itself. The entire architecture is built on the premise of homeostasis as repair, and it forces us to reconsider how we view the perfection of the human body. Flyxion leverages the concept of satisficing versus optimizing. Take that a step further, because that distinction is the philosophical core of this entire deep dive. In classical engineering, and in the legacy ODE models, the goal is optimization. You write an equation to find the perfect, mathematically ideal state for a variable. You want the system to rest at the absolute peak of efficiency. But biology doesn't do that. No. Biology does not operate on optimization. It operates on satisficing. It searches for a state that is simply good enough to prevent death. The body isn't a divine, perfectly calibrated machine. It is a landlord slapping duct tape on a leaking pipe, just to keep the drywall from collapsing. Exactly. An organism doesn't care about perfection. It cares about remaining within bounds. The Physiome engine doesn't evaluate every possible repair operator to find the one that yields the most mathematically elegant future state. It just grabs the first one that works. It simply grabs the first combination of operators that successfully pulls the variable back inside the admissibility boundary, and then it stops. It executes the bare minimum required to keep the lights on. By building a software architecture that only ever asks, is this state admissible, and deliberately refuses to ask, is this the best possible state? Flyxion hasn't just written a better piece of code. They have created a structural reflection of biological reality. The code architecture is practically indistinguishable from the biological philosophy. We have covered massive ground today. Usually, when we try to model a physical system, right, we rely on this kind of assumption of mathematical perfection.
Right, yeah, like everything can be solved with a neat little equation.
Exactly. Like if you want to simulate a rocket trajectory, you map out all the physics, you know, thrust, gravity, atmospheric drag,
and you just drop it all into this massive interconnected system of equations.
Because the math basically dictates every single millisecond of the flight.
Right, and for a really long time, the assumption in computational biology has been that you can treat the human body the exact same way.
Which is wild.
It is wild. The idea that if you just write enough equations, you can perfectly mathematically calculate a human being.
Yeah, but I mean, that assumption completely breaks down when you actually look at how biology functions on the ground.
Which brings us to the core of what we are getting into today. Welcome to the Dean Dive, everyone.
We are so glad you are here with us.
Today, our mission is to basically rewire how you think about simulating the human body.
We're unpacking this fascinating August 2026 paper by an independent researcher named Flyxion.
Yeah, the paper is called Physiome, well, technically Physiome, an Admissibility and Repair Driven Architecture for Whole Body Physiological Simulation.
It's a mouthful. But the stated mission of this text is to just completely tear down that old body is a rocket ship paradigm.
It really fundamentally changes the premise of what the body is trying to do.
Because a biological system is not optimizing some beautiful global mathematical equation.
Right.
It's frantically patching leaks.
Yes. The author proposes this radical software architecture that says, hey, stop treating human biology as a mathematically optimized supercomputer.
Instead, let's model it as a tired property manager who is just trying to fix whatever happens to be broken in the building right now.
That is exactly it. But, you know, to understand the magnitude of what Flyxion is proposing here, we really have to establish what this new architecture is replacing.
Right. The old way of doing things.
Exactly. So the paper contrasts its new approach with the traditional, what they call, evolution-first paradigm.
Oh, evolution-first.
Yeah. And the gold standard for that traditional approach is this simulation environment called HUMOD.
HUMOD.
Right. Developed at the University of Mississippi Medical Center.
And HUMOD is this absolute marvel of classical modeling.
I mean, it attempts to simulate the human organism by tracking several thousand interdependent variables.
So we're talking, like, everything. Cardiovascular output, renal filtration rates.
Hendocrine hormone concentrations. You name it. It's all in there.
But the critical part, and what the paper really focuses on, is how it tracks them.
It links all of those thousands of variables together using ordinary differential equations.
ODEs.
Right. It relies heavily on the mathematics of continuous change.
Which is that evolution-first method you mentioned.
Exactly. The philosophical pathway there is mechanism leading to behavior.
So in the HUMOD architecture, every single variable in the entire organism has a defined derivative at every single instant of time.
Meaning, they're all changing all the time based on each other.
Yes. You just have one massive mutually coupled system of equations.
And the software uses a single global time step to basically integrate every variable simultaneously.
Okay. Let's ground that for someone writing the code, or just for you listening right now.
If I am programming HUMOD, and I have a set of variables, let's say, heart rate, blood volume, and kidney filtration rate.
Okay.
Because they are coupled ODEs, the math dictates that the future state of my blood pressure, say, one millisecond from now,
is a strict continuous function of the rates of change, all those other variables happening right now.
Right. The system constantly calculates the evolution of everything all at once.
Even if nothing is actually happening.
Exactly. Even if nothing physiologically interesting is happening to a specific variable at that exact moment,
the global integration just forces the math forward.
Right.
And while that produces simulators with, you know, very high clinical fidelity for known scenarios,
it creates an absolute combinatorial nightmare under the hood.
When a programmer tries to expand it, right?
Yeah.
Yeah. Or debug it.
Let's really dig into that combinatorial nightmare, because the paper makes a really strong point about the scaling limits of ODEs.
Right. Like, if I want to add a completely new system to Humid, say, I discover a new hormone,
or I want to add a new synthetic drug to the simulation, I can't just drop that single new variable into the code base and let it run, can I?
Oh, absolutely not.
No, because you are dealing with a coupled mathematical matrix.
To add that single new hormone, you must define exactly how its derivative interacts with the differential equations for heart rate and blood pressure and renal clearance.
You essentially have to rewrite or at least adjust the equations for every single biological system that the new hormone touches.
That sounds exhausting.
It is. As you add more variables, the complexity of managing those mathematical intersections just grows exponentially.
The correctness of your entire simulation becomes tied to the correctness of this single, massive, tightly coupled web of calculus.
So it's like, okay, it's like trying to simulate the traffic grid of a major city, right?
But you're doing it by calculating the exact physics, the tire friction, the velocity, and the gravitational pull of every single car at every millisecond.
Right, rather than just looking at the traffic light.
Exactly. And the moment you introduce one new car onto the highway, the simulation has to completely recalculate the collision probability and aerodynamic drag for every other car on the grid simultaneously.
And if you type a plus sign instead of a minus sign somewhere in that giant matrix of equations...
Boom.
Yeah, the entire city's traffic pattern collapses.
And finding that one typo requires unraveling the entire coupled system.
Which is just awful.
It's the structural limitation that Flyxion is explicitly targeting.
Physiome steps away from us entirely. I mean, it completely abandons the differential equations.
It throws out the evolution-first paradigm.
Yes. Instead, physiome introduces an admissibility-first architecture.
Admissibility-first. Which means the flow of the program totally changes, right?
Instead of mechanism leading to behavior, the physiome flow is boundary, leading to violation, leading to repair, leading to behavior.
That's the loop.
Instead of asking what differential equation governs a variable's constant evolution, the physiome engine asks a much simpler question.
Which is?
What range of values is the rest of the organism willing to accept from this variable?
Oh, the guardrails?
Precisely. You define an acceptable upper and lower bound for a variable.
So, a blood pressure range.
A sodium-level range.
And the system just holds the variable in its current state completely static until that boundary is violated.
So it's not constantly doing math on it?
Not at all.
The engine fundamentally does not tear how the variable arrived at that state.
There is no derivative driving it forward.
Only when a violation is detected does the system trigger a repair mechanism.
I want you to think about your own body for a second, just sitting there listening to this.
If you sit still, is your biological hardware actively running complex calculus to determine what your core temperature should be exactly five seconds from now?
Definitely not.
Right.
Or is it largely passive, just waiting for a biological sensor to trip when you get too cold?
The paper argues that biological homeostasis is much closer to that second option.
Yeah, it's reactive.
A baroreceptor in your neck isn't computing a target mean arterial pressure for the future.
It just fires when the current pressure slips outside the acceptable operating window right this second.
And the software architecture of Physiome is attempting to perfectly mirror that biological reality.
I mean, it is a system built entirely on detecting out-of-bound states and deploying local fixes.
But to execute that, without the rigid structure of a massive ODE matrix, you need an incredibly robust engine, right, to manage the chaos of all those local fixes.
Oh, absolutely.
Because waiting for a warning light to flash on a dashboard only works if the code actually knows how to interpret the alarm.
And, more importantly, it needs to know how to prioritize them.
Right.
If my kitchen is on fire but my basement is also flooding, the property manager needs a very clear set of rules for who to dispatch first.
So how does this engine actually operate when multiple systems start failing?
So the core of the Physiome simulator is this really elegant loop.
It consists of three phases, detect, rank, and dispatch.
Detect, rank, dispatch. Got it.
And this is all handled by a domain-independent engine, meaning it doesn't know anything about biology.
It just knows about data contracts.
Okay, so it's just pure logic at that level.
Right.
So the detection phase relies on what the code base calls the constraint module.
This module basically assigns an admissibility boundary to a variable.
Which is just the acceptable upper and lower numerical limits like we talked about.
It is, but it also contains a really crucial mathematical component called the severity function.
Oh, this is fascinating. Tell them about the severity function.
So when the engine scans the variables, the severity function evaluates their current state.
If a variable is sitting comfortably inside its admissibility boundary, the function just returns a zero.
No alarm.
Right, no alarm.
But if the value drifts outside the bound, the function returns a magnitude that is proportional to how far outside the bound it currently sits.
But wait, if we are looking at the entire human organism, the engine has to compare vastly different metrics, right?
Like how do you rank the severity of a blood pressure that is 5 millimeters of mercury too high against a plasma sodium concentration that is 5 mil equivalents per liter too low?
That is the big question.
Because the units are totally different.
And their natural scales in the body are completely unrelated.
That is where the normalization process inside the severity function becomes just absolutely vital.
The engine doesn't just pass along the raw numerical error.
It takes that raw deviation and divides it by the width of the admissible interval for that specific variable.
Okay, hold on.
Let's map out that math for a second so I can visualize it.
If my acceptable blood pressure range is mathematically defined as say 30 units wide and my current pressure is 5 units over the limit, my severity score is 5 divided by 30, so roughly 16%.
But if my acceptable sodium range is much tighter, say only 10 units wide, and I'm off by that same raw number of 5 units, then the severity score is 5 divided by 10.
That's a 50% severity.
Exactly.
So even though the raw error is identical, just the number 5, the engine immediately recognizes that the sodium violation is a much more severe breach of its specific biological contract.
That is exactly how it works.
The engine effectively normalizes every single crisis in the body onto a common unitless scale.
It's essentially asking which variable is furthest outside its own specific tolerance.
That is so smart.
So once it has calculated these normalized severity scores for every single violation currently happening in the organism, the engine ranks them.
From most severe to least severe.
Okay, so the ranking is complete.
The engine knows the sodium crisis is the biggest fire in the building right now.
What triggers the fix?
Well, it moves to the repair module, which utilizes these data structures called repair ops or repair operators.
A repair op.
Yeah. A repair op is a discrete piece of code with three components.
First, it has a name.
Second, it has a predicate, which is basically a Boolean rule that checks if this specific operator is a valid candidate to fix the current type of violation.
Like true or false, can I fix this?
Exactly.
And finally, it contains a pure function.
And what does that do?
That pure function takes the current state of the organism, looks at the violation, and outputs an entirely new updated state of the organism intended to pull the variable back toward the boundary.
So the engine detects the sodium violation, normalizes it, ranks it at the top of the queue, and searches its library of repair operators for one whose predict it matches low sodium.
Yep.
And then it applies the pure function to shift the organism's state.
You've got it.
But, you know, the paper outlines this really fascinating gap in this logic.
It details certain variables that are explicitly classified as observable but not repairable.
What is that about?
Yeah.
The code base currently includes constraints on variables like gastric pH, you know, stomach acid levels, and cardiovascular heart rate.
Okay.
These variables have defined admissibility boundary parameters.
The engine is constantly watching them, and the severity function will absolutely flag them if they go out of bounds.
But the author deliberately hasn't built any repair operators for them yet.
Correct.
There is literally no operator in the current code base whose predicate matches a heart rate violation or a gastric pH violation.
Which means the engine detects a massive failure, normalizes it, ranks it as the highest priority, searches the repair library, finds absolutely nothing, logs the violation to the system output, and just moves on to the next problem.
Yeah.
It literally just watches the system fail and takes notes.
Which sounds kind of terrible, like a glitch.
It sounds like a glitch, but it's actually a testament to the robustness of that detect rank dispatch loop.
I mean, the engine doesn't crash because a biological response is missing.
It behaves deterministically.
Oh, I see.
It handles the absence of a repair just as gracefully as the presence of one, which is really critical when you are incrementally building an organism from scratch.
You can't have the whole thing crash just because you haven't coded the stomach acid fix yet.
Okay.
That makes sense.
Yeah.
But let me push back on the architecture of this dispatch loop for a second, specifically how it handles multiple simultaneous crises.
Sure.
If the engine takes this list of normalized violations, ranks them, and then searches for a fix for the single worst one, doesn't that create a huge biological bottleneck?
How do you mean?
Well, if a patient is in severe trauma, say, they're hemorrhaging blood volume, which is a massive cardiovascular violation.
Yeah.
But they also have a systemic infection triggering a severe cytokine violation.
Right.
A real human body doesn't just pick the blood loss, fix it completely, and then later decide to start working on the infection.
The body initiates multiple compensatory responses at the exact same time.
That's true.
So if this software engine just plucks the highest severity score and ignores the rest, it's kind of failing to simulate biology, isn't it?
You've identified the exact architectural hurdle the author ran into during the early development of the engine.
Oh, really?
Yeah.
Initially, the dispatch function was written exactly as you feared.
It evaluated the highest round violation, found the first repair operator that matched it, applied that single fix, and just stopped the loop.
Which didn't work.
The author quickly realized this resulted in severe physiological inaccuracies because, as you pointed out, a single derangement often provokes multiple simultaneous responses.
So how do you write a software loop that allows a single-threaded engine to fix multiple things at once without creating absolute chaos in the underlying data?
Well, the author resolved this by rewriting the dispatch function to utilize a concept formalized in the paper as sequential composition.
Sequential composition.
Right.
So now, when the engine isolates a violation, it doesn't just stop at the first matching repair operator.
It scans the entire library and aggregates every single operator whose predicate matches that specific violation.
Ah, okay.
So if there is a massive pathogen load triggering a cytokine violation, it finds the immunoresolution operator,
but it keeps looking and also grabs, like, the fever response operator.
Yes, exactly.
And instead of trying to apply them simultaneously, which would risk data collision if both operators tried to modify the same variable at the exact same millisecond,
the engine applies them sequentially within the boundary of a single simulation tick.
Okay, walk me through that tick.
It feeds the organism state into the first operator, takes the resulting new state, and instantly feeds that new state into the second operator.
So it chains them together.
Yep.
To the outside observer and to the simulation's timeline, both repairs happen simultaneously to combat the single violation.
That is a really elegant way to solve the bottleneck of multiple repairs for a single violation.
But introducing the idea of a single simulation tick brings up an even more complex issue, which the paper dives into.
The flow of time.
The flow of time itself.
Because if we look at the reality of human biology, these repair mechanisms do not operate on the same temporal scale.
Not even remotely.
Right.
A cardiovascular bariflex, adjusting your heart rate, takes roughly one second.
Right.
But the hepatic system, clearing a toxin from the liver, takes minutes, or even hours, and a full reproductive cycle takes nearly a month.
Exactly.
How does the physio architecture handle the vast disparities in how fast these systems run, especially since it completely abandoned the global time step of the ODE models?
This is one of the most significant structural departures in the paper.
It's detail as the abandonment of global time.
Okay.
So in the HUM and ODE paradigm, every variable across the entire organism is tethered to one master clock.
One clock to rule them all.
Right.
For the mathematics of numerical integration to remain stable, basically, to prevent the equations from spiraling out of control, that global clock must tick at the speed of the absolute fastest dynamic in the entire model.
Right.
Because if you set the master clock to tick every five minutes, you would completely miss the one second cardiovascular reflexes.
Miss it entirely.
The simulation would calculate a heart rate, go to sleep for five minutes, and wake up to find the patient had flatlined.
Yes, exactly.
So you are forced to make the master clock tick every single second.
But doing that means you are forcing the slow-moving renal system, which might only need to update its filtration status every 15 minutes,
to constantly recalculate its massive set of equations every single second, just because the heart requires it.
Which is a huge waste of processing power.
Flyxion argues that this synchronization is a numerical convenience rather than a fact about the organism.
It is an artifact of the math, not a reflection of biology.
Right.
To put it in perspective, the ODE method is kind of like an orchestra, where the conductor forces every musician to play the exact same frenetic tempo.
I like that analogy.
So the tuba player, representing the slow renal system, is forced to play a frantic note every single second,
just because the snare drummer, the cardiovascular system, is playing a fast beat.
Yes.
It's incredibly inefficient.
Physiome entirely decentralizes this.
It introduces what they call the continuation trait.
The continuation trait.
Yeah.
In this architecture, there is no global time step driving the biology.
Instead, every individual subsystem declares its own required interval,
basically the number of seconds it wants to wait before it is advanced again.
Ooh.
So the cardiovascular system registers itself with a one-second interval.
Right.
The nervous system runs on a two-second interval, the hepatic system declares a 10-minute interval,
and the renal system declares a 15-minute interval.
Exactly.
The engine utilizes a central scheduler.
This scheduler simply maintains a ledger of the next fire time for each subsystem.
It looks at the ledger, finds whichever subsystem is scheduled to fire next,
advances that specific subsystem's internal state by its requested interval,
and then reruns the detect rank dispatch loop to see if that advancement caused any violations.
So it never forces the liver to advance, just because the heart did.
Never.
Giving every organ its own sheet music sounds structurally brilliant.
But if we extrapolate the computational reality of this, there's a catch, right?
There is a catch.
Because the paper lays out a roadmap for level three development,
which involves pushing the simulation down to the cellular and molecular scale.
We're talking about gene regulation and intracellular signaling pathways.
Systems that operate on timescales of milliseconds or even microseconds.
Exactly.
If you introduce a cellular subsystem that requests to be advanced every single millisecond,
doesn't that cripple the entire engine?
Because even though the liver isn't being advanced every millisecond,
the central scheduler still has to run its detection loop to check for violations every time any clock ticks.
Yeah, you are hitting on a massive computational bottleneck,
which the author explicitly acknowledges in their discussion of big O complexity.

//! PhysiologicalState: struct-of-structs, one block per subsystem's
//! observables. Immutable by convention — transitions produce a new
//! PhysiologicalState rather than mutating an existing one.

use crate::constraint::{
    collect_violations, detect_violations_for, detect_violations_for_stateful, Violation,
};
use crate::subsystems::cardiovascular::CardiovascularState;
use crate::subsystems::endocrine::EndocrineState;
use crate::subsystems::gi::GiState;
use crate::subsystems::hematologic::HematologicState;
use crate::subsystems::hepatic::HepaticState;
use crate::subsystems::immune::ImmuneState;
use crate::subsystems::integumentary::IntegumentaryState;
use crate::subsystems::lymphatic::LymphaticState;
use crate::subsystems::metabolic::MetabolicState;
use crate::subsystems::microcirculation::MicrocirculationState;
use crate::subsystems::musculoskeletal::MusculoskeletalState;
use crate::subsystems::nervous::NervousState;
use crate::subsystems::renal::RenalState;
use crate::subsystems::reproductive::ReproductiveState;
use crate::subsystems::respiratory::RespiratoryState;
use crate::subsystems::skeletal::SkeletalState;
use crate::subsystems::thermal::ThermalState;

#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq)]
pub struct PhysiologicalState {
    pub cardiovascular: CardiovascularState,
    pub renal: RenalState,
    pub hepatic: HepaticState,
    pub gi: GiState,
    pub nervous: NervousState,
    pub immune: ImmuneState,
    pub hematologic: HematologicState,
    pub endocrine: EndocrineState,
    pub metabolic: MetabolicState,
    pub respiratory: RespiratoryState,
    pub thermal: ThermalState,
    pub microcirculation: MicrocirculationState,
    pub lymphatic: LymphaticState,
    pub musculoskeletal: MusculoskeletalState,
    pub integumentary: IntegumentaryState,
    pub skeletal: SkeletalState,
    pub reproductive: ReproductiveState,
    pub t: f64,
}

impl PhysiologicalState {
    pub fn baseline() -> Self {
        PhysiologicalState {
            cardiovascular: CardiovascularState {
                mean_arterial_pressure: 90.0,
                heart_rate: 70.0,
                stroke_volume: 70.0,
            },
            renal: RenalState {
                gfr: 100.0,
                plasma_sodium: 140.0,
                plasma_volume: 5.0,
                functioning_mass: 1.0,
                perceived_perfusion_pressure: 90.0,
            },
            hepatic: HepaticState {
                bilirubin: 0.6,
                albumin: 4.2,
                ammonia: 20.0,
                clotting_factors: 1.0,
            },
            gi: GiState {
                gastric_ph: 2.0,
                motility_index: 1.0,
                luminal_glucose: 0.0,
                absorption_rate: 0.5,
            },
            nervous: NervousState {
                sympathetic_tone: 0.3,
                parasympathetic_tone: 0.4,
                baroreceptor_gain: 1.0,
            },
            immune: ImmuneState {
                wbc_count: 7.0,
                cytokine_level: 0.05,
                crp: 1.0,
            },
            hematologic: HematologicState {
                hemoglobin: 14.5,
                platelet_count: 250.0,
                coagulation_index: 1.0,
            },
            endocrine: EndocrineState {
                insulin: 10.0,
                cortisol: 12.0,
                aldosterone: 10.0,
            },
            metabolic: MetabolicState {
                blood_glucose: 90.0,
                lactate: 1.0,
            },
            respiratory: RespiratoryState {
                blood_ph: 7.40,
                paco2: 40.0,
                pao2: 95.0,
            },
            thermal: ThermalState { core_temp: 37.0 },
            microcirculation: MicrocirculationState {
                tissue_perfusion: 38.0,
                oxygen_extraction: 0.30,
                capillary_leak_index: 0.25,
            },
            lymphatic: LymphaticState {
                lymph_flow: 2.2,
                interstitial_volume: 12.2,
                edema_index: 0.35,
            },
            musculoskeletal: MusculoskeletalState {
                muscle_workload: 0.2,
                fatigue_index: 0.2,
                glycogen_reserve: 90.0,
            },
            integumentary: IntegumentaryState {
                barrier_integrity: 0.92,
                skin_perfusion: 0.7,
                sweat_rate: 0.2,
            },
            skeletal: SkeletalState {
                ionized_calcium: 1.22,
                remodeling_signal: 1.0,
                marrow_output: 1.0,
            },
            reproductive: ReproductiveState {
                sex_hormone_index: 1.0,
                cycle_phase: 0.5,
                placental_flow: 1.0,
            },
            t: 0.0,
        }
    }
}

pub fn all_violations(state: &PhysiologicalState) -> Vec<Violation> {
    collect_violations(vec![
        detect_violations_for(&state.cardiovascular),
        detect_violations_for_stateful(&state.renal, &state.renal),
        detect_violations_for(&state.hepatic),
        detect_violations_for(&state.gi),
        detect_violations_for(&state.nervous),
        detect_violations_for(&state.immune),
        detect_violations_for(&state.hematologic),
        detect_violations_for(&state.endocrine),
        detect_violations_for(&state.metabolic),
        detect_violations_for(&state.respiratory),
        detect_violations_for_stateful(&state.thermal, state),
        detect_violations_for(&state.microcirculation),
        detect_violations_for(&state.lymphatic),
        detect_violations_for(&state.musculoskeletal),
        detect_violations_for(&state.integumentary),
        detect_violations_for(&state.skeletal),
        detect_violations_for(&state.reproductive),
    ])
}

use std::collections::HashMap;

use crate::constraint::{AdmissibilityBoundary, Constraint, ObservableBoundary, Violation};
use crate::repair::{Continuation, Inputs, Perturbation, RepairOp};
use crate::specification::{ObservableSpec, RepairSpec, SubsystemSpecification};
use crate::state::PhysiologicalState;

#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq)]
pub struct LymphaticState {
    pub lymph_flow: f64,          // L/day equivalent
    pub interstitial_volume: f64, // L
    pub edema_index: f64,         // relative
}

pub fn boundary() -> AdmissibilityBoundary {
    HashMap::from([
        ("lymph_flow", Constraint::new(1.0, 4.0)),
        ("interstitial_volume", Constraint::new(11.0, 14.5)),
        ("edema_index", Constraint::new(0.0, 1.0)),
    ])
}

impl ObservableBoundary for LymphaticState {
    fn observables(&self) -> HashMap<&'static str, f64> {
        HashMap::from([
            ("lymph_flow", self.lymph_flow),
            ("interstitial_volume", self.interstitial_volume),
            ("edema_index", self.edema_index),
        ])
    }

    fn boundary() -> AdmissibilityBoundary {
        boundary()
    }

    fn subsystem_name() -> &'static str {
        "lymphatic"
    }
}

fn lymph_pump_augmentation_apply(state: &PhysiologicalState, _v: &Violation) -> PhysiologicalState {
    let mut next = state.clone();
    next.lymphatic.lymph_flow += 0.3;
    next.lymphatic.interstitial_volume = (next.lymphatic.interstitial_volume - 0.1).max(11.0);
    next.lymphatic.edema_index = (next.lymphatic.edema_index - 0.08).max(0.0);
    next
}

pub fn lymph_pump_augmentation() -> RepairOp {
    RepairOp {
        name: "lymph_pump_augmentation",
        applies_to: |v| {
            v.subsystem == "lymphatic"
                && (v.variable == "edema_index" || v.variable == "interstitial_volume")
        },
        apply: lymph_pump_augmentation_apply,
        writes: &[
            "lymphatic.lymph_flow",
            "lymphatic.interstitial_volume",
            "lymphatic.edema_index",
        ],
    }
}

pub struct LymphaticClock;

impl Continuation for LymphaticClock {
    fn interval(&self, _current: &PhysiologicalState) -> f64 {
        120.0
    }

    fn advance(
        &self,
        state: &PhysiologicalState,
        _dt: f64,
        inputs: &Inputs,
        perturbation: &Perturbation,
    ) -> PhysiologicalState {
        let mut next = state.clone();
        let micro_leak = next.microcirculation.capillary_leak_index * 0.05;
        let thermal_drive = (next.thermal.core_temp - 37.0).max(0.0) * 0.03;
        let muscle_pump = 0.12 * inputs.exercise_intensity;
        let stochastic = perturbation.sample(next.t, "lymphatic") * 0.01;

        next.lymphatic.interstitial_volume += micro_leak + thermal_drive - muscle_pump;
        next.lymphatic.lymph_flow = (next.lymphatic.lymph_flow + muscle_pump + stochastic).max(0.8);
        next.lymphatic.edema_index = (next.lymphatic.interstitial_volume - 11.0) / 3.5;
        next
    }
}

const OBSERVABLES: [ObservableSpec; 3] = [
    ObservableSpec {
        name: "lymph_flow",
        unit: "L/day",
        admissible_lo: 1.0,
        admissible_hi: 4.0,
    },
    ObservableSpec {
        name: "interstitial_volume",
        unit: "L",
        admissible_lo: 11.0,
        admissible_hi: 14.5,
    },
    ObservableSpec {
        name: "edema_index",
        unit: "relative",
        admissible_lo: 0.0,
        admissible_hi: 1.0,
    },
];

const REPAIRS: [RepairSpec; 1] = [RepairSpec {
    subsystem: "lymphatic",
    name: "lymph_pump_augmentation",
    triggers: &["lymphatic.edema_index", "lymphatic.interstitial_volume"],
    reads: &[
        "microcirculation.capillary_leak_index",
        "musculoskeletal.muscle_workload",
    ],
    writes: &[
        "lymphatic.lymph_flow",
        "lymphatic.interstitial_volume",
        "lymphatic.edema_index",
    ],
}];

pub fn specification() -> SubsystemSpecification {
    SubsystemSpecification {
        subsystem: "lymphatic",
        clock_interval_seconds: 120.0,
        observables: &OBSERVABLES,
        repairs: &REPAIRS,
    }
}
